Millionaire Shoes by Lee Arnold (2020) Shares insights on building wealth through real estate investing and entrepreneurship. Go-Givers Sell More by Bob Burg and John David Mann (2010) Highlights how generosity and focusing on value create success in sales and business. The 7 Habits of Highly Effective People by Stephen R. Covey (1989) A timeless guide to personal and professional effectiveness through key habits. The Power of Habit by Charles Duhigg (2012) Explains how habits work and how they can be changed to achieve success. The Miracle Morning by Hal Elrod (2012) Introduces a morning routine to improve focus, productivity, and overall well-being. What Got You Here Won’t Get You There by Marshall Goldsmith (2007) Offers strategies to overcome personal and professional roadblocks to reach the next level. Excellence Wins by Horst Schulze (2019) Lessons in leadership and customer service from a cofounder of the Ritz-Carlton. Life is a Series of Presentations by Tony Jeary (2004) A guide to mastering communication and presentations for maximum impact. Start With Why by Simon Sinek (2009) Explores the importance of identifying your "why" to inspire others and drive success. Jack Welch and the GE Way by Robert Slater (1999) Chronicles Jack Welch’s transformative leadership at General Electric. The Millionaire Next Door by Thomas J. Stanley and William D. Danko (1996) Reveals the surprising habits and traits of America’s wealthy. Reading these books provides valuable insights into personal development, leadership, and strategic thinking, essential for success in both business and life. They cover a range of topics, from building effective habits and understanding decision-making to mastering communication, fostering customer relationships, and achieving financial independence. By learning from the experiences and expertise of successful leaders, entrepreneurs, and behavioral experts, you gain practical tools to overcome challenges, inspire others, and continuously grow. The Lee Arnold System Page 24 What is a Power Team? A Power Team is a group of people with complementary professions. They work with the same clients, but do not take business away from each other. A great example is easily found in the real estate industry. A realtor, mortgage broker, building inspector, title agency, real estate attorney, private money lender, and private money broker all service clients looking to purchase property. SETTING UP YOUR BUSINESS What can your Power Team do? Create a dynamic team of investors from the people that you already know, like, and trust Improve and expand each other’s business Create borrower “hunters” or “bird dogs” to find steady flow of borrowers Bring professionals in from all areas affected by your business to give expert advice and support Creating a Power Team Your Power Team needs to help with three things: Find deals Find money Close deals Already have your power team in mind? Create a Power Team by Becoming “The Connector” SETTING UP YOUR BUSINESS Places to Network – Online and Offline Social Media REI Clubs Seminars Digital Meetings Blogs Referrals Networking The Lee Arnold System Page 26 KNOW THE PLAYERS - KNOW YOUR ROLE The Lee Arnold System Page 27 Private Money Lending is a loan from a private individual to a non-owner-occupied real estate investor, secured by real estate as collateral. Rates and terms for these types of loans will vary by lender and depend upon the experience level of the borrower, the length of an investor’s relationship with a particular lender and the perceived risk of the deal. There are very few hard and fast rules to private money. What is a Private Money Loan? The Major Players: KNOW THE PLAYERS - KNOW YOUR ROLE Borrower The Borrower: Who is the Private Money Borrower? Someone who: Needs quick funding for time sensitive loans May have been denied a bank loan, for any reason, including credit and excessive loan conditions Wants to avoid the long hassle of processing a bank or institutional loan Needs a larger loan with more flexible terms than most banks offer (continued on next page) Broker Lender Notes: The Lee Arnold System Page 28 Wants an opportunity to make an investment in a new property using the equity in owned real estate – cross-collateralization. Has circumstances making it difficult to obtain institutional loans: Complex financing structures (LLC’s, partnerships, trusts, corps) Credit problems (minor to moderate) Bankruptcy (old or current) Property held in probate, trusts, family limited partnerships, irrevocable trusts, corporations, etc. Divorce Why People are Borrowing Private Money KNOW THE PLAYERS - KNOW YOUR ROLE Qualifying Challenges: Institutional lenders are very picky about the borrower and property. Private Lenders look at: Appraised value Borrower experience Property equity Exit strategy Need for Speed: Institutional lenders normally take 60-90 days to fund a transaction. Private Lenders: Review the property’s merit Require less paperwork Can complete a loan in as little as 72hrs - 3wks More Money, More Often: Institutional lenders normally lend a capped amount of money on a capped amount of loans. Private Lenders can lend: Multiple loans on multiple properties More money so borrowers can use less of theirs With far fewer seasoning requirements The Lee Arnold System Page 29 Investment Parameters: Institutional lenders look at the as-is nature of the property and borrower. They have no imagination on future value. Private Lenders lend on: Amount of “skin in the game” Equity Exit strategy (highest and best use) Property’s cash flow capability or income potential KNOW THE PLAYERS - KNOW YOUR ROLE Notes: Purchasing a fixer-upper to repair and flip in three to six months Purchasing a fixer-upper to repair and keep as a rental property Refinancing to invest money in other cash-flow opportunities Most Private Borrowers Fit Into These Categories The Lee Arnold System Page 30 KNOW THE PLAYERS - KNOW YOUR ROLE The Broker: Who is the Private Money Broker? An intermediary who brings non-owner-occupied commercial real estate investors, borrowers, and private money institutions together but does not use its own funds to originate private money mortgages gathers paperwork from these borrowers and oversees the loan submission and packaging process for submission to the lender for underwriting, approval, and funding. The mortgage funds are then lent in the name of the private money institution. Upon closing, the private money broker receives an origination fee, often 1-3%, from the borrower as compensation for his/her services. This is paid from the HUD. What is your WHY? A very high-income potential The smallest start-up investment Providing a service that is continually needed The pleasure of helping people A recession-proof business A year-round service (not seasonal) People actually flock to you No refunds, no losses Points paid out of closing Cash transactions (no receivables) A service well-accepted and revered by lenders and borrowers Personal and financial freedom A more fulfilling life More family time More travel time A broader, more dynamic experience Why People are Brokering Private Money? The Lee Arnold System Page 31 Follow a checklist (get this from your lender). Make sure all information is ordered. Make sure all information is complete. NO White-Out! Make sure all information is accurate. Make sure the copies & faxes are readable. Submit everything within 48 hours of the borrower’s request. KNOW THE PLAYERS - KNOW YOUR ROLE Introduction – Get the initial story & vet the deal/client. Consult – Create a workable deal. Commitment to Deal – Set expectations. Make sure the client has buy-in. Get your broker fee agreement signed! Prepare the Presentation – Pick your lender & assemble file for that lender. Submission – Submit a full, complete package to the lender. Monitor Progress – Help keep the process going. Make sure everyone is moving forward. Follow Up – Keep the communication lines open between yourself, the lender, and the borrower. Loyalty is the most difficult of the customer service goals to achieve. Once you have it, you have something your competition will never have… The next order! The Lee Arnold System Page 33 KNOW THE PLAYERS - KNOW YOUR ROLE The Lender: Know your products. Review your commitments. Follow up daily with clients. Follow up after funded loans. Turn down files that don’t fit yours or the lender’s criteria (don’t waste your time on Timewasters.) Follow Up and Follow Through Notes: Who is the Lender? Someone who is frustrated with losses in the stock market and is sick and tired of low returns from traditional investments like CDs, bonds, and mutual funds. Someone who does not want the hands-on approach associated with owning real estate and wants a much more passive option. Many lenders simply do not want the headache and liability that comes along with owning real estate. Someone who wants more control over their financial portfolio and to direct their investments. The Lee Arnold System Page 34 KNOW THE PLAYERS - KNOW YOUR ROLE Healthy returns with shorter terms - Many investment vehicles, like stocks and bonds, are earning lower than desired, or simply unpredictable returns. They also tie your money up for longer periods of time. Private money mortgages are a solid source of healthy returns in a shorter period of time. Tangible investments - Unlike investing in stocks and bonds, when you invest in private money mortgages you are investing in tangible assets. We vet out all the properties and make sure they are the best opportunities for your dollars. Hands off - It’s a passive investment. We do all the heavy lifting for you. We research, review, assemble and provide you, the lender, multiple private money mortgage opportunities until you identify the one that is within your level of comfort. It creates ongoing, residual income over the life of the loan. It can earn, on average, 8-12 percent annualized return. Protection - It’s in the first trust deed position. Control - Lenders can choose the parameters of their investments. Safety: Requires “skin in the game” from the borrower (in the form of cash, partner, collateral, and/or equity). To gain the gratification of investing in real people and real opportunities!!! It can streamline your due diligence process with automated property data retrieval, document management to help organize and manage your property research information efficiently. SendFuse provides: Market analysis data, including information on local property market trends, recent sales data in the area, comparable properties, and other market insights Neighborhood information, such as crime rates, school ratings, amenities, demographics, and other relevant details about the surrounding area Historical data on the property, such as past sales transactions, ownership history, and any recorded liens or encumbrances Evaluation of the property's financial performance Another important step in the due diligence process is to determine if there is equity in the subject property. This is by obtaining a title report through SendFuse. With a title report, you can read through a property's transaction history, including loan and liens that have been placed against the subject property. You can also obtain other details such as comparable homes and a map of the homes that surround the subject property. The SendFuse title report will even include the name and mailing address of the owner of the subject property. Note: if the address of the owner and the address of the subject property match, this means that the subject property is owner occupied. If there is equity in the subject property, we will move forward and pursue the homeowner to do business. SendFuse SENDFUSE The Lee Arnold System Page 54 Interpreting the Debt Stack Move on to Zillow. Do a search of the address in Zillow to get a Zestimate (a comps-based estimate). Next, use the SendFuse Title Report (Property Profile Report) will provide you with a great deal of useful information on a property, including: Current owner Property characteristics Most recent loan and sale Assessment and taxes Previous mortgages, refinances, and sales Title changes Map with comps Area sales analysis Nearby next sellers and out-of-state owners Determining equity using the debt stack requires a little sleuthing, but if you take the time to do the math, you can get a good idea. Once you have identified a potential investment, our goal is to find out the history, or the "story" of a property to determine equity. We want to answer the following questions: What is the property worth? What is owned on the property? How much can I sell it for? Always start your property research with Google. Do a search of the address in Google; if the first page of Google has no red flags (criminal activity, fires, etc.), we're good. SENDFUSE The Lee Arnold System Page 55 Let's take a look at this example, located at 28 Pine Rd, Millville NJ. After running a search, Google has no negative stories on the first page. Running a Zillow search results in photos, a description, neighborhood evaluation, map and lot lines, other pertinent information, and a Zestimate of $67,600. Now look at the Zillow "Lot Lines" view of the property that includes rooftop values. This will allow you to come up with a ballpark estimate of value. Interpreting the Debt Stack SENDFUSE The Lee Arnold System Page 56 To get the "story" from the Title Report, we always start at the bottom of the Resale list and work our way up to the current owner. Make note of every transaction date, the amount, the financing, the type of sale, and who are the buyers and sellers. On some reports, there may be multiple pages of transaction records while others may have few or just one record. We can see on our property the current owner is Mr. Parliman. This property's most recent sale has a transfer date of April 10, 2014, for $30,000 from Mr. Crane, using an attorney to transfer title. Next, use SendFuse to run the Research Property and the Title For reports. Take a moment to examine the owner information, property characteristics, recent sale, and assessed taxes. To calculate equity, the key items you'll want to use in the report are: Recording Date Transfer Value and Transfer Amount Type of Sale Buyers and Sellers Transaction Flags Note: The first time you read a title report, it may seem confusing, but familiarizing yourself with the terminology will make it easier for you each time you research. Interpreting the Debt Stack SENDFUSE The Lee Arnold System Page 57 The next (and most current as we noted in the Recent Sale & Loan section) Resale item is recorded April 5, 2014 from Mr. Crane to Mr. Parliman. The purchase amount is $30,000 in a cash transaction, by attorney, so it appears there is no mortgage. This resale history is relatively simple and straightforward. To build the history of this property, we go to the end of the Resale records. The oldest and first recorded item in the Resale list is a cash transaction, by attorney (Ba), of $25,095 on October 7, 2005 from a Mr. Lewis to Mr. Crane. Step 1 Add the following 3 items together: Purchase Price (PP), Rehab (R), and After Repaired Value (ARV). PP + R + ARV = Step 2 Multiply the Total from #1 by 10% - This is the Estimated Closing and Selling Cost. (#1 total) x 0.1 = ECSC Step 3 Add the total from #2 to the Purchase Price (PP) and Rehab (R) to get the Estimated Total Cost. ECSC + PP + R = ETC Step 4 Subtract the Estimated Total Cost in #3 above from the After Repaired Value (ARV). ARV – ETC = Estimated Net Profit Step 5 This will equal the Estimated Net Profit without holding and utility cost. 10% Rule Steps Example A “good” deal is defined by an Estimated Net Profit of at least 20K. ·Purchase Price: $75,000 ·Rehab: $35,000 ·After Repaired Value: $150,000 Step 1 $75,000 + $35,000 + $150,000 = $260,000 Step 2 $260,000 x 0.1 (10%) = $26,000 Step 3 $26,000 + $75,000 + $35,000 = $136,000 Step 4 $150,000 - $136,000 = $14,000 The Estimated Net Profit is $14,000 DEAL OR NO DEAL Notes: The Lee Arnold System Page 62 State = Kansas (SFR) Purchase Price = $80,000 Rehab = 0 LEADS, TALKING TO BORROWERS The Lee Arnold System Page 69 Leads are what you get from your website or referrals. Prospects are what they become once you’ve opened the lines of communication. YES, you will have to talk to them! This business is all about developing relationships! VETTING LEADS, TALKING TO BORROWERS What’s the Difference Between a Lead and a Prospect? It’s okay to knock a lead off the list if they are wasting your time and energy. Only spend time on High Probability Prospects! Disqualifying Characteristics: The Victim The Angry Curser The “Maybe” Lead - A “maybe” turns into “no” more often than it turns into a “yes.” The most significant cost in a real estate professional’s business isn’t the advertising or marketing. It’s the opportunity cost of making an investment in the wrong person and not getting paid, when you could have worked with someone who would have generated a broker fee. Leads to Disqualify Notes: The Lee Arnold System Page 70 Respect Your Position - By answering a sales phone call, you are essentially taking the entire reputation of your company on your shoulders. The phone call is often your first impression and you only have one shot at that. Take it seriously. Ask Many Questions - People are talkers and many, for the most part, enjoy talking about themselves or their "situation." Allowing a prospective client to talk will change your success rate. There are also many good things that can come from a great conversation with a new person. Ask questions to best answer their needs, and you’ll be surprised where it leads the relationship above and beyond a closed loan. Be a Sales Consultant, Not a Salesperson - Studies show that when someone is labeled a salesperson, their chances of being well received drop dramatically. However, consultants are adored members of our society. Who doesn’t like someone offering free advice? Be the expert they need, and they’ll gratefully do business with you. Speak in Benefits, Not Features - As exciting as it is for you to have the #1 service in the Industry, as an Independent Private Money Broker, your prospect wants to know how it’s going to improve his or her life or business. Speak in terms of benefits to the customer. No one likes a braggart, but everyone loves someone who can do something for them. Know Your Stuff - Be prepared: Have a ready, effective, benefit-driven answer prepared. Do some brainstorming around likely questions. VETTING LEADS, TALKING TO BORROWERS When the Phone Rings & When You Call… Notes: The Lee Arnold System Page 71 Focus on the person in front of you (not on your phone or others around you). Don’t interrupt: let the person finish before you begin. Pay special attention to key points (write them down to remember them). Reflect back what you think you heard. Watch/listen for non-verbal cues. Ask relevant questions, especially those that help them articulate their pain. If in-person, respond physically: lean in and nod when appropriate. VETTING LEADS, TALKING TO BORROWERS Listen Your Way Into a Sale Your Purpose Within the Call Identify their need. Identify their pain if that need is not met. Figure out if and how your solution will meet their needs and eliminate the pain. If it does, articulate the benefits they will get by using your product to solve their needs. Converse with them. Don’t grill them. Notes: The Lee Arnold System Page 72 How did you hear about me? In case we get disconnected, what is your best phone number? Is the deal under contract? How many deals have you done so far? How many deals have you done in the last year? What do you do for a living? Do you have other forms of income outside real estate? How much did you make on your last deal? Why are you buying this property? How much do you plan to make on this project? How much of your own money are you investing into this project? How much did you make on your last deal? How much do you need to borrow? To what email address should I send the application information? What will the loan funds be used for? How quickly do you need/want to close? What is your purchase price? How much do you expect to need to spend on repairs? What will the property be worth when you have repaired it? What is the property worth today, as it stands? How did you determine those amounts? These are all woven into a friendly conversation, not asked sequentially without comments in between. In a sales contract, the parties are the seller(s) and the buyer(s). Seller must be the person(s) or entity listed as vested owner on title. If there are any real estate agents brokering the sale, they are typically listed also as the real estate brokers/agents who would earn the commission from the sale. Identify the real estate (property): At least the address, but preferably the legal description must be on the contract. Identify the purchase price: The amount of the sales price or a reasonably ascertainable figure (an appraisal to be completed at a future date) must be on the contract. Include signatures: A real estate contract must be entered into voluntarily (not by force), and must be signed by the parties, to be enforceable. Have a legal purpose: The contract is void if it calls for illegal action. Involve competent parties: Mentally impaired, drugged persons, etc. cannot enter into a contract. Contracts in which at least one of the parties is a minor are voidable by the minor. Zero Tolerance Loan Fraud Policy Our first line of defense for loan fraud This form requires the borrowing entity’s name to be filled in, dated, and signed by the member/ manager/ officer/ owner of entity and all guarantors. Any violations to this policy will result in being placed on the List of Ineligible Borrowers with the company. Notes: The Lee Arnold System Page 133 APPLICATION TO CLOSING Understanding Contingencies Contingencies are conditions which must be met if a contract is to be performed. Contingencies that suspend the contract until certain events occur are known as "suspensive conditions." Contingencies that cancel the contract if a certain event occurs are known as "resolutive conditions." Most contracts of sale contain contingencies of some kind or another. It is possible for a real estate contract not to have any contingencies. Note time frames specified, if any Types of Contingencies Some types of contingencies which can appear in a real estate contract include: Mortgage contingency - Performance of the contract (purchase of the real estate) is contingent upon or subject to the buyer getting a mortgage loan for the purchase. Usually such a contingency calls for a buyer to apply for a loan within a certain period after the contract is signed. Since most people require financing to complete their purchase, mortgage contingencies are one of the most common. If the financing is not secured, the buyer may unilaterally cancel the contract by stating that his or her condition has not or will not be satisfied or allow the contract to expire by declining to waive the condition within the specified time. Inspection contingency - Purchase of the real estate is contingent upon a satisfactory inspection of the real property revealing no significant defects. Contingencies could also be made on the satisfactory repair of a certain item associated with the real estate. Another sale contingency - Purchase or sale of the real estate is contingent on a successful sale or purchase of another piece of real estate. The successful sale of another house may be needed to finance the purchase of a new one (owner occupancy red flag). Appraisal contingency - Purchase of the real estate is contingent upon the contract price being at or below a fair market value determined by an appraisal. Lenders will often not lend more than a certain percentage (fraction) of the appraised value, so such a contingency may be useful for a buyer. Notes: The Lee Arnold System Page 134 APPLICATION TO CLOSING Understanding Date of Closing A typical real estate contract specifies a date by which the closing must occur. The closing is the event in which the money (or other consideration) for the real estate is paid for and title (ownership) of the real estate is conveyed from the seller(s) to the buyer(s). The conveyance is done by the seller(s) signing a deed for buyer(s) or their attorneys or other agents to record the transfer of ownership. Often other paperwork is necessary at the closing. The date of the closing is normally also the date when possession of the real estate is transferred from the seller(s) to the buyer(s). The contract may have provisions in case the seller(s) hold over possession beyond the agreed date. Understanding Addendums Any changes to buyer’s financing contingencies, closing dates, etc. must be done through an addendum and signed by all parties. Understanding Earnest Money Earnest money deposit from the buyer(s) usually accompanies an offer to buy real estate. The amount is a small fraction of the total price and is listed in the contract with the remainder of the cost to be paid. Typically, the earnest money deposit represents "consideration." If no other type of consideration is given, earnest money is required to fulfill this element of the contract. Notes: The Lee Arnold System Page 135 APPLICATION TO CLOSING Title Policy Understanding Title Policy Title insurance is principally a product developed and sold in the United States because of an alleged comparative deficiency of the U.S. land records laws. Title insurance is meant to protect an owner's or a lender's financial interest in real property against loss due to title defects, liens or other matters. It will defend against a lawsuit attacking the title as it is insured or reimburse the insured for the actual monetary loss incurred, up to the dollar amount of insurance provided by the policy. What is a Title Search? A detailed examination of the historical records concerning the property The purpose of the search is to verify the seller’s right to transfer title (purchase transactions) or the borrower’s vested interest in the property (refinance). This information is gathered through county auditors, county and city treasurers, and the Federal Bankruptcy Court. Title Policies There are two types of policies - owner and lender. Just as lenders require fire insurance and other types of insurance coverage to protect their investment, nearly all institutional lenders also require title insurance (a loan policy) to protect their interest in the collateral of loans secured by real estate. Although some mortgage lenders, especially non-institutional lenders, may not require title insurance. For Cogo Capital, title insurance must be from one of the eight approved national companies. Buyers purchasing properties for cash or with a mortgage lender often want title insurance (an owner policy) as well. A loan policy provides no coverage or benefit for the buyer/owner and so the decision to purchase an owner policy is independent of the lender's decision to require a loan policy. Notes: The Lee Arnold System Page 136 APPLICATION TO CLOSING Lender’s Policy This is sometimes called a loan policy and it is issued only to mortgage lenders. It follows the assignment of the mortgage loan, meaning that the policy benefits the purchaser of the loan if the loan is sold. For this reason, these policies greatly facilitate the sale of mortgages into the secondary market. Appraisals: Each lender will have their own process for this. A typical process will include a completed request for appraisal through the lender. The lender will order the appraisal, and the appraisal company will contact the broker/ borrower to schedule the time and collect payment. If the borrower chooses to move forward with an appraisal vs. the AVM, current COGO Capital policy is to order an ARV and as-is appraisal on all rehab loans (scope of work and budget required to complete this!) Ordering the Appraisal Notes: The Lee Arnold System Page 137 APPLICATION TO CLOSING Closing The Cogo closer will be notified by the funding department that an investor has committed to a loan file. The Cogo closer will notify the broker that the loan has been committed to, when the funds will be coming in, and when the closing can be scheduled. The broker will then be required to schedule the closing with the client and get closing info to the Cogo closer: when will the closing occur? Who is doing the closing? The Cogo closer will be verifying that any prior-to requirements have been met (quit claim deeds, hazard insurance, special info required by title company such as tax payoffs, tax certifications, water bills, etc.) The Cogo closer will be drawing documents, verifying all information from borrower, and actually sending closing package to the escrow company or closing agent. Escrow will return estimated HUD for closing to the Cogo closer. Estimated HUD will be reviewed to ensure that final numbers are correct for closing costs. Once HUD is approved, the Cogo closer will initiate the wire and release funds to closing for actual closing procedure. The Cogo closer will wait for original documents to come back and will verify that all documents have been signed and all info is correct and authorize the recording. All closing questions from closing agent need to be referred to the Cogo closer - NOT to the broker. The documents that you are responsible for, when you submit a typical loan file, are noted in the list below. Introduction: Instructions: You will complete this assignment as part of your homework. 5REHUW 1RQH 'RFX6LJQ(QYHORSH,'()&&%$ A9 A-10 Initial _______ Vϲ-0ϯϭϱ201ϲ Zero Tolerance Loan Fraud Policy ^ĞĐƵƌĞĚ/ŶǀĞƐƚŵĞŶƚ,ŝŐŚzŝĞůĚ&ƵŶĚ͕>> utilizes a sophisticated suite of advanced software tools alongside stringent underwriting procedures to combat loan fraud and identity theft. NOTICE: ANY SUBMISSION OF INFORMATION TO ^hZ/Es^dDEd,/',z/>&hE͕>> KNOWINGLY CONTAINING FALSE INFORMATION IN CONNECTION WITH A PRIVATE MORTGAGE LOAN/FIRST TRUST DEED APPLICATIONS IS A FEDERAL CRIME Mortgage fraud means a material misstatement, misrepresentation, or omission relied upon ^ĞĐƵƌĞĚ/ŶǀĞƐƚŵĞŶƚ,ŝŐŚzŝĞůĚ &ƵŶĚ͕>> to fund or purchase-or not to fund or purchase-a mortgage. Such mortgage fraud included, but is not limited to, false information contained in identification and employment documents, false mortgagee or mortgagor identity, and fraudulent appraisals; theft of custodial funds, non-remitted payoff funds, misrepresentations of borrower funds, and property flipping where designed to falsely inflate property value. Possible mortgage fraud means^ĞĐƵƌĞĚ/ŶǀĞƐƚŵĞŶƚ,ŝŐŚzŝĞůĚ&ƵŶĚ͕>> has a reasonable belief, based upon a review of information available, that mortgage fraud me be occurring or has occurred. It is the intent of ^ĞĐƵƌĞĚ/ŶǀĞƐƚŵĞŶƚ,ŝŐŚzŝĞůĚ &ƵŶĚ͕>>’s Zero Tolerance Loan Fraud Policy (“Policy”) to support the industry’s and law enforcement’s efforts to eradicate residential Private Mortgage Loan/First Trust Deed Fraud. Accordingly, by doing business with ^ĞĐƵƌĞĚ/ŶǀĞƐƚŵĞŶƚ,ŝŐŚzŝĞůĚ &ƵŶĚ͕>>, the Borrower, Broker, Realtor shall be directly responsible for the actions performed in the course of doing business with ^ĞĐƵƌĞĚ/ŶǀĞƐƚŵĞŶƚ,ŝŐŚzŝĞůĚ&ƵŶĚ͕>> and for maintaining compliance with this Policy. Although loan fraud may be perpetrated in many forms, some of the most common examples are shown below. x Identity theft x Submission of inaccurate or misleading information, including false statements on loan application(s) and falsification of documents purporting to substantiate credit, employment, deposit and asset information or personal information including identity, ownership/non-ownership of the real property; x The alteration or forgery of otherwise predominately accurate information; x Inaccurate representations of current occupancy or intent to maintain required occupancy as agreed in the security instrument; x Lack of due diligence or concern by borrower, realtor, broker, loan officer, interviewer or processor, including failure to obtain or divulge all information required by the application and failure to request further information as dictated by Borrower’s response to other questions. This could include the following examples: (i) simultaneous or consecutive processing of multiple owner-occupied loans from single applicant where information differs on each application; (ii) permitting an applicant or interested third party to assist with the processing of the loan; and (iii) failure to disclose any relevant or pertinent information known to the Broker which could negatively impact the lending decision. ^ĞĐƵƌĞĚ/ŶǀĞƐƚŵĞŶƚ,ŝŐŚzŝĞůĚ&ƵŶĚ͕>> specifically represents and warrants the quality and integrity of its loan production to ^ĞĐƵƌĞĚ/ŶǀĞƐƚŵĞŶƚ,ŝŐŚzŝĞůĚ&ƵŶĚ͕>>’s investors. Consequently, loans containing fraud or material misrepresentations that negatively impact ^ĞĐƵƌĞĚ/ŶǀĞƐƚŵĞŶƚ,ŝŐŚzŝĞůĚ&ƵŶĚ͕>>Ζs business reputation can severely strain investor relationships. The potential consequences for knowing participants of this practice are also very serious and ^ĞĐƵƌĞĚ/ŶǀĞƐƚŵĞŶƚ,ŝŐŚzŝĞůĚ&ƵŶĚ͕ >>’s position is a zero tolerance policy. For Brokers/Realtors: x Criminal prosecution, which could result in fines, imprisonment or both. x Revocation of Broker’s license. x Loss of access privileges to lenders resulting from the exchange of legally permissible information between lenders, mortgage insurance companies; FHLMC, FNMA and other investors; policy agencies; and state and federal regulatory agencies including the Department of Justice and the FBI. x Loan repurchase and/or liability to ^ĞĐƵƌĞĚ/ŶǀĞƐƚŵĞŶƚ,ŝŐŚzŝĞůĚ&ƵŶĚ͕>> for resulting monetary loss. x Civil action by borrower, ^ĞĐƵƌĞĚ/ŶǀĞƐƚŵĞŶƚ,ŝŐŚzŝĞůĚ&ƵŶĚ͕>> and/or other parties involved in the transaction. x Loss of approval status with ^ĞĐƵƌĞĚ/ŶǀĞƐƚŵĞŶƚ,ŝŐŚzŝĞůĚ&ƵŶĚ͕>> The undersigned does hereby acknowledge receipt of this notice and agrees to comply with conditions set forth herein. I further declare that the amounts in Part I above are the amounts from my electronic income tax return. I consent to allow my intermediate service provider, transmitter, or electronic return originator (ERO) to send my return to the IRS and to receive from the IRS (a) an acknowledgement of receipt or reason for rejection of the transmission, (b) the reason for any delay in processing the return or refund, and (c) the date of any refund. If applicable, I authorize the U.S. Treasury and its designated Financial Agent to initiate an ACH electronic funds withdrawal (direct debit) entry to the financial institution account indicated in the tax preparation software for payment of my federal taxes owed on this return and/or a payment of estimated tax, and the financial institution to debit the entry to this account. This authorization is to remain in full force and effect until I notify the U.S. Treasury Financial Agent to terminate the authorization. To revoke (cancel) a payment, I must contact the U.S. Treasury Financial Agent at 1-888-353-4537. Payment cancellation requests must be received no later than 2 business days prior to the payment (settlement) date. I also authorize the financial institutions involved in the processing of the electronic payment of taxes to receive confidential information necessary to answer inquiries and resolve issues related to the payment. I further acknowledge that the personal identification number (PIN) below is my signature for my electronic income tax return and, if applicable, my Electronic Funds Withdrawal Consent. Write the Operating Agreement: Follow the steps to craft an operating agreement that addresses your LLC's exact needs 2. 3.Sign the Document: All the members of the LLC should sign the operating agreement 4.File the Operating Agreement Securely: Keep a copy of the signed agreement for your records Documents Needed to File and Run an LLC Internal Revenue Service (IRS) Form SS-4: Obtain an Employer Identification Number (EIN) for your business via IRS Form SS-4 1. 2.Name Reservation Application: Some states require you to reserve your business name Articles of Organization: This document is filed with your state and includes basic information about your LLC 3. 4.Operating Agreement: This document outlines the rules and procedures of your LLC 5.Initial and Annual Reports: Some states require these reports to keep your LLC in good standing Tax Registrations: Depending on your state and the nature of your business, you may need to register for certain taxes 6. Business Licenses: Depending on your state and the nature of your business, you may need certain licenses to operate 7. Please note that the exact process and requirements can vary by state, so it's important to check with your state's Secretary of State or a legal professional to ensure you're meeting all the necessary requirements. Also, consider consulting with a tax advisor to understand the best taxation option for your LLC. A Acceleration clause: A provision in a contract that permits the creditor to demand immediate payment of the entire outstanding balance if the debtor fails to meet specific conditions or defaults on the agreement. Addendum: An additional document or section appended to a contract or agreement to modify, clarify, or supplement its terms. ADU (Accessory Dwelling Unit): A secondary residential unit located on the same property as the main dwelling, often used for rental purposes or as a separate living space for family members. AITD (All-Inclusive Trust Deed): A type of mortgage that includes existing liens and encumbrances, allowing the buyer to assume the responsibility for the entire debt, including the original loan and any additional amounts financed by the seller. Allonge: An attachment to a negotiable instrument, such as a promissory note, used to endorse or add additional terms to the original document. Amortization: The process of gradually paying off a debt over time through regular, scheduled payments, which cover both the principal and interest. Appraisal: A professional assessment or evaluation of the value of a property, asset, or item, conducted by a licensed appraiser. Appreciation: The increase in value of a property, asset, or investment over time, often influenced by factors such as market demand and economic conditions. Arbitrage: The practice of taking advantage of price differences or inefficiencies in the market to buy a property at a lower price and quickly resell it for a profit. ARM (Adjustable-Rate Mortgage): A type of mortgage loan with an interest rate that can change periodically based on predetermined factors, such as market conditions, usually resulting in fluctuating monthly payments for the borrower. Arrear: An amount of money that is overdue and unpaid, typically used in the context of late payments on debts. ARV (After Repair Value): The estimated value of a property after it has undergone necessary repairs or improvements. Assignment of contract: The transfer of rights and obligations of a contract from one party (the assignor) to another party (the assignee), often used in real estate transactions or when assigning contractual obligations to a third party. GLOSSARY The Lee Arnold System Page 383 Assumption: A buyer taking over the existing mortgage of a property from the seller, thereby assuming responsibility for the loan and its terms. Auction: A public sale conducted by the lender or trustee to sell the foreclosed property to the highest bidder, allowing the lender to recover the outstanding debt and the winning bidder to acquire the property. Authorization to Release Information (ARI): A legal document signed by an individual granting permission to a specified party to access and release certain personal or confidential information. B Balloon term/pmt: A type of loan or payment arrangement where the borrower makes small regular payments over time, but the remaining balance becomes due in a lump sum (balloon payment) at the end of the loan term. Bargain and sale deed: A type of real estate deed that conveys the property from the seller (grantor) to the buyer (grantee) without guaranteeing that the property is free from encumbrances or liens. Birddog: An individual who helps investors find potential deals or properties, often receiving a fee or commission for their assistance in locating suitable opportunities. Blended funding: A financing strategy that combines multiple sources of funding, such as loans and equity investments, to meet the financial needs of a project. BPO (Broker Price Opinion): An evaluation or estimate of a property's value, performed by a licensed real estate agent or broker. Bridge financing: A short-term loan or financing option that provides immediate funds to bridge a financial gap until a more permanent or long-term financing solution is secured. C Capital: Financial resources, assets, or funds used to invest in and operate a business, support projects, or acquire assets. Capital gains: The profit or increase in the value of an investment or asset when its selling price exceeds its original purchase price, typically subject to taxation. Capitalization rate: Also known as cap rate, it is a measure used to evaluate the potential return on an investment property by dividing its net operating income by its current market value. Closing costs: Expenses incurred by both the buyer and seller during the process of transferring ownership of a property, including fees for loan processing, title insurance, appraisal, and other related costs. Cash-on-Cash Return (CoC): A metric used to assess the profitability of an investment property by comparing the cash flow generated to the amount of cash invested in the property. GLOSSARY The Lee Arnold System Page 384 Collateralization: The process of providing assets or securities as collateral to secure a loan or other financial obligation, which serves as a form of protection for the lender in case of default. Commercial: Refers to properties that are used primarily for business, investment, or income-generating purposes, such as office buildings, retail centers, industrial warehouses, hotels, and apartment complexes, rather than for residential or personal use. Comps: Short for "comparables," comps refer to recently sold properties with similar characteristics (location, size, features, etc.) that are used to determine the market value of a property, typically in real estate appraisals. Contingency: A condition or stipulation in a contract that must be satisfied for the agreement to be binding. If the contingency is not met, the contract may be canceled without any penalty. Conventional mortgage: A traditional home loan that is not insured or guaranteed by a government agency (such as FHA or VA), typically requiring a higher down payment and stricter credit requirements compared to government-backed loans. Certificate of Occupancy (COO): A document issued by a local government or building authority, indicating that a property has met all the necessary building codes and is safe for occupancy. Credit bid: A bid made by the creditor at a foreclosure auction, using the amount of the outstanding debt as payment to acquire the property. CRM (Customer Relationship Management): A system or software used by businesses to manage and track interactions with customers, including sales, marketing, and customer service activities. CTA (Call to Action): A prompt or directive typically used in marketing or advertising to encourage the audience to take a specific action, such as clicking a link, making a purchase, or subscribing to a newsletter. Current Value: The present market or appraised value of an asset, property, or investment at a specific point in time. Customer Avatar: A detailed profile or representation of an ideal customer, including demographics, preferences, behaviors, and interests, used to tailor marketing strategies and communications. D Debt Stack: The hierarchy or order of different debts or loans secured by a property, representing the priority in which they will be repaid in case of a default or foreclosure. Deed of Trust: A legal document used in some states instead of a mortgage to secure a loan for the purchase of real estate, typically involving three parties: the borrower (trustor), the lender (beneficiary), and a neutral trustee who holds the property's title until the debt is fully paid. GLOSSARY The Lee Arnold System Page 385 Deed-in-lieu/Cash for keys: A process in real estate where a borrower facing foreclosure voluntarily transfers the property's ownership to the lender to avoid foreclosure. Depreciation: The decline in the value of an asset, such as real estate, over time due to factors like wear and tear, obsolescence, or market conditions. Disclosure state: A state in the U.S. where sellers are legally required to disclose specific information about a property's condition or history to potential buyers during a real estate transaction. Discounting (notes): The process of selling a promissory note or other debt instrument at a price lower than its face value, resulting in the buyer acquiring the debt at a discounted rate. Double close: A real estate transaction involving two separate closings, where the investor (middle person) buys a property from the seller and then immediately sells it to the end buyer in the same day, often used in wholesale real estate deals. Draw process/reimbursement draws: The procedure by which a construction or development project receives periodic payments or reimbursements based on the completion of specific milestones or work stages, allowing for the ongoing funding of the project as it progresses. Due on Sale clause: A provision in a mortgage or loan agreement that allows the lender to demand full repayment of the loan if the borrower sells, transfers, or conveys the property to another party without the lender's consent. E-F Earnest money: A deposit made by a potential buyer to demonstrate their serious intention to purchase a property, usually paid when making an offer, and held in escrow until the closing of the transaction. Equity: The difference between the market value of an asset (such as a property) and the amount owed on any outstanding loans or mortgages secured by the asset. It represents the ownership value or stake of the owner in the asset. Equity deal: A type of investment or financial arrangement where one party provides capital or resources in exchange for an ownership stake or share of profits in a real estate venture. Escalation clause: A provision in a contract, often in real estate transactions, that allows for the price or specific terms of the agreement to be adjusted upwards or downwards based on certain conditions, such as changes in market conditions. Escrow: A financial arrangement where a neutral third party (usually an escrow agent or company) holds and manages funds, documents, or assets on behalf of two parties involved in a transaction until all conditions of the agreement are met. FHA (Federal Housing Administration): A government agency in the United States that insures mortgages and provides mortgage insurance to lenders, making it easier for individuals with lower down payments or credit scores to qualify for home loans. GLOSSARY The Lee Arnold System Page 386 H HELOC (Home Equity Line of Credit): A revolving line of credit that allows homeowners to borrow against the equity in their property, using their home as collateral. It operates similarly to a credit card, with a predetermined credit limit and a variable interest rate. Holding costs: The ongoing expenses incurred by a property owner while holding or owning a property, including mortgage payments, property taxes, insurance, maintenance, and utilities. Home equity loan: A loan secured by the equity in a homeowner's property, where the borrower receives a lump sum of money and repays the loan in regular installments with interest over a fixed term. HUD statement: Also known as the HUD-1 Settlement Statement, it is a document provided to the buyer and seller at the closing of a real estate transaction in the United States. It itemizes all the charges and credits associated with the closing, including loan fees, escrow items, and other costs. I-J Inspection: An evaluation or examination of a property's condition, conducted by a professional inspector or appraiser, to identify any potential issues or defects before a real estate transaction or as part of a property maintenance process. Institutional loan: A loan provided by a traditional financial institution, such as a bank, credit union, or mortgage lender, to individuals or businesses, usually at competitive interest rates and subject to standard lending practices. Interest: The cost of borrowing money, expressed as a percentage of the loan amount, which is paid by the borrower to the lender as compensation for the use of the borrowed funds. Judicial foreclosure: The legal process of foreclosing on a property through the court system, where a lender initiates foreclosure proceedings and the court oversees the sale of the property to recover the outstanding debt. L LCE (Loan Cost Estimate): A detailed breakdown of the estimated costs associated with obtaining a mortgage loan, including loan origination fees, appraisal fees, title insurance, and other related expenses. This document is typically provided to borrowers during the mortgage application process to help them understand the costs involved in obtaining the loan. Lease with option to purchase: A real estate agreement that combines elements of a lease and a purchase option, allowing a tenant to rent a property with the option to buy it at a predetermined price and within a specified period. Leverage: The use of borrowed funds or financial instruments to increase the potential return on an investment or to amplify potential gains or losses. Lien: A legal claim or encumbrance on a property that serves as security for a debt or obligation, giving the lienholder the right to take ownership of the property if the debt is not repaid. GLOSSARY The Lee Arnold System Page 387 Lien position: The order in which liens are ranked in terms of priority when multiple liens exist on a property. The lien with the highest priority is generally the first to be paid off in the event of a property sale or foreclosure. Lien release: A legal document issued by the lienholder stating that a lien on a property has been satisfied and the property is no longer encumbered by the lien. Lienholder: The individual or entity that holds a legal claim (lien) on a property as collateral for a debt or obligation owed by the property owner. Lis pendens: A notice filed in public records to inform potential buyers and interested parties that a lawsuit is pending regarding the ownership or title of a property. LLC (Limited Liability Company): A type of business entity that provides limited liability protection for its owners (members) while offering flexibility in management and taxation. Loan analyst: A professional responsible for evaluating loan applications, analyzing creditworthiness, and determining the risk associated with lending money to borrowers. They assess financial documents, credit history, and other factors to make informed decisions about loan approvals. Loan processor: A professional responsible for collecting and organizing the necessary documents and information for mortgage loan applications and guiding the loan through the underwriting process. Loan officer: A financial professional who works for a lender or financial institution and is responsible for assisting borrowers in the loan application process, evaluating their financial situation, and recommending suitable loan options. LOI (Letter of Intent): A formal written document expressing the intention of one party to engage in a business transaction or negotiation with another party, outlining the basic terms and conditions of the proposed deal. LTR (Long Term Rental): Refers to the practice of renting out a property to tenants for an extended period, typically for a year or more, as opposed to short-term rentals or vacation rentals. LTV (Loan-to-Value ratio): The ratio between the loan amount and the appraised value or purchase price of a property, expressed as a percentage. It is used by lenders to assess the risk of a loan and the borrower's equity in the property. M MAO (Maximum Allowable Offer): In real estate investing, the highest price an investor is willing to pay for a property, taking into consideration the potential repair and holding costs, desired profit margin, and market conditions. MFH (Multi-Family Housing): Referring to properties or buildings with multiple housing units designed for accommodating more than one family or household. MLS (Multiple Listing Service): A database or platform used by real estate agents and brokers to share information about properties that are available for sale or rent, allowing them to cooperate and facilitate transactions. GLOSSARY The Lee Arnold System Page 388 P-Q Partial assignment: Refers to the transfer of only a portion of the rights or interest in the note to another party, while the original lender retains ownership of the remaining portion. Performing note: A loan or debt instrument where the borrower is making timely payments as agreed, and it is considered to be in good standing with no significant risk of default. PITI (Principal, Interest, Taxes, and Insurance): An acronym used in the context of mortgage payments, representing the four components of a typical monthly mortgage payment: Power of Sale/Non-judicial/Statutory: A type of foreclosure process in which the lender or mortgage holder can sell the property without going through the court system. This method is authorized by state statutes and allows for a quicker and less costly foreclosure process compared to judicial foreclosures. Private money: Funds provided by individuals, private investors, or private institutions, rather than traditional financial institutions, to finance real estate investments or transactions. Private money broker: An intermediary who connects borrowers seeking private money loans with private investors or lenders willing to provide the financing. Private mortgage loan: A mortgage loan that is funded by a private individual or entity, usually for real estate transactions, and is not backed or insured by a government agency. Probate: The legal process by which a court oversees the distribution of a deceased person's assets, including real estate, to their heirs or beneficiaries, as outlined in the person's will or by applicable laws of intestacy if there is no will. Promissory note/Mortgage note: A legal document that outlines the terms of a property loan, including the borrower's promise to repay the debt and the details of the repayment schedule. The property serves as collateral for the loan. Proof of funds letter: A document provided by a bank or financial institution to confirm that a person or entity has sufficient funds available to complete a financial transaction, such as a real estate purchase. Purchase and sales agreement (P&S): A legally binding contract between a buyer and seller outlining the terms and conditions of a real estate transaction, including the purchase price, contingencies, and closing date. Quit claim: A legal document used to transfer an individual's interest or claim in a property to another party, but without providing any warranty or guarantee about the property's title. R Receiver: A person or entity appointed by the court to take control of and manage a property or business entity when it is in financial distress or facing foreclosure. Redemption period: The period during which a borrower can redeem their property from foreclosure by paying the outstanding debt, interest, and associated costs. Refinance Cash-out: A mortgage refinance where the borrower takes out a new loan that is greater than the existing loan balance, allowing them to receive the difference in cash. GLOSSARY The Lee Arnold System Page 389 Rehab: Short for rehabilitation, it refers to the process of renovating or improving a property, used in real estate investment to increase its value. REO (Real Estate Owned): (also OREO) A term used to describe properties that are owned by a lender or financial institution after foreclosure. Re-performing note: A previously delinquent loan that has become current again, with the borrower resuming regular payments. Retail: In the context of real estate, it refers to the sale or lease of properties to individual consumers or endusers. ROI (Return on Investment): A financial metric used to measure the profitability of an investment, expressed as a percentage of the initial investment. RoR (Rate of Return): Similar to ROI, it represents the gain or loss on an investment relative to the initial investment amount. S Sandwich lease purchase: A real estate investing strategy where an investor leases a property from the owner and then subleases it to a tenant-buyer with the option to purchase. Scope of Work: A detailed description of the work to be performed in a construction or renovation project. Seasoning: The length of time a loan or investment has been active or in existence. Self-directed IRA: An individual retirement account that allows the account holder to have more control over their investment choices, including real estate and other alternative assets. Seller carry/Seller financing: A financing arrangement where the property seller acts as the lender and provides financing to the buyer instead of requiring a traditional mortgage from a bank. Servicing: The management and administration of a loan on behalf of the lender, including collecting payments, managing escrow accounts, and handling customer service inquiries. Servicing history: The records and documentation of the payment history and other relevant information related to a loan or mortgage. SFH (Single-Family Home): A residential property designed and intended for one family to occupy. Sheriff's sale: A public auction of a property conducted by the sheriff or a court-appointed official as part of the foreclosure process. Short sale: A real estate transaction in which the lender agrees to accept less than the full amount owed on a property as payment in order to avoid foreclosure. Simultaneous close: Also known as a double closing, it refers to two separate real estate transactions that occur simultaneously, where a property is bought and then immediately sold to another buyer. GLOSSARY The Lee Arnold System Page 390 Skiptrace: The process of locating a person's current contact information, often used in debt collection or finding individuals who are difficult to locate. Special warranty deed: A type of deed that conveys a property from the grantor to the grantee and includes specific warranties or guarantees related to the title, but only for the time the grantor owned the property. Staging: The process of preparing a property for sale by arranging and decorating it to make it more appealing to potential buyers. STR (Short-Term Rental): A rental property or accommodation that is rented out for a short duration, typically on a daily or weekly basis, such as vacation rentals. Strict foreclosure: A foreclosure process in which the lender takes ownership of the property without a public auction or sale, usually allowed in certain states. Subject to: A term used in real estate transactions to indicate that the buyer is assuming the existing mortgage or loan on the property without formally assuming liability for it. Subprime paper: Refers to loans or debt instruments that are considered riskier and have higher interest rates due to the borrower's lower creditworthiness. Sub-servicing: When a primary servicer outsources some or all of its servicing functions to another company or entity. T Term: The duration or length of time for which a loan or investment will be in effect or active. Three-tiered offer/3-Tiered Offer: A type of real estate offer that includes three different prices based on different contingencies or conditions. Title insurance: An insurance policy that protects the buyer and lender against losses due to defects in the property's title. Title report: A document that provides a detailed history of a property's ownership, liens, and other encumbrances. U-V Under-performing note: A loan where the borrower is making irregular or partial payments, indicating a higher risk of default. Underwriting: The process of evaluating the creditworthiness and risk of a borrower to determine whether to approve a loan. UPB (Unpaid Principal Balance): The outstanding amount of the principal on a loan that remains to be paid. USP (Unique Selling Proposition): The unique features or benefits of a product or service that differentiates it from competitors and attracts customers. Virtual assistant: A remote administrative assistant who provides administrative support services to clients or businesses from a remote location. GLOSSARY The Lee Arnold System Page 391 W-Y Wholesale: In real estate, it refers to purchasing properties at a discounted price and selling them to other investors or buyers without making any significant improvements or renovations. Wholesaler: A person or company that engages in the practice of wholesaling, which involves buying properties at a discount and selling them to other investors or buyers for a profit. wMAO (Wholesale Maximum Allowable Offer): The maximum price that a real estate wholesaler is willing to pay for a property based on the property's after-repair value (ARV) and potential repair costs. Wrap: A type of seller financing arrangement where the seller provides financing to the buyer, and the buyer makes payments to the seller while the seller continues to pay their existing mortgage. Yield: The return or earnings generated from an investment, typically expressed as a percentage of the investment amount.
